man

person · 72 works · 488 quoted facts · 189 connections

Abstract written by a model

In The Book of Job, man is a frail and mortal creature, yet capable of remarkable practical skill while lacking wisdom apart from fear of the Lord. In Psalms: Children of men, examined by the LORD looking down to find any who understand and seek God, are found all gone aside, together corrupt, with no one doing good. In Psalms: Humanity is portrayed as fleeting: like a breath and a walking shadow, busying itself in vain and heaping up wealth for unknown heirs. In Psalms: Man is the psalmist's subject of wonder: why the LORD cares for him. In Ecclesiastes or, The Preacher, the sons of men are mortal beings sharing the animals’ fate, limited in power and knowledge, and urged to rejoice and enjoy their labor. In Ecclesiastes or, The Preacher, man is a vain and frail creature whose duty is to fear God and keep His commandments because every work will be judged. In The Wisdom of Solomon: Humankind was formed by God's wisdom to rule creatures and the world in holiness and righteousness. In The Wisdom of Jesus the Son of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus: The sons of Adam, humanity collectively, bear a heavy yoke and great travail from birth until burial in the earth, troubled by fear of the future and death; one is warned against confiding in him.

and 91 more works

In The Wisdom of Jesus the Son of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus: Man was made by the Lord from the beginning and left with free choice in his own counsel. In The Wisdom of Jesus the Son of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus, mankind is created in the Lord’s image, given knowledge and moral law, yet remains mortal and returns to earth and ashes. In The Wisdom of Jesus the Son of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus: Men, like Adam, originate from the ground; the Lord holds them like clay in a potter's hand, blessing some and cursing others, making their ways different. In Paul’s First Letter to the Corinthians: Man is portrayed as head of woman, with Christ as his head, and as God's image and glory. In 1 Timothy: are_desired_to pray [in every place, lifting up holy hands without anger and doubting] Men are included among all people for whom prayer is to be made, and God desires their salvation. In The Letter to the Hebrews: is_made_lower_than angels [a little lower, crowned with glory and honor] Quoting a psalm, the text says man was made a little lower than the angels, crowned with glory and honor, and had all things put under his feet, though not yet seen so. In Surah 2: The Cow (Al-Baqara): Mankind is Allah’s creation, called to worship Him and avoid rivals. In Surah 2: The Cow (Al-Baqara): is_performed_by Allah Allah creates mankind and those before them and appoints a viceroy in the earth. In Surah 3: The Family of Imraan (Aal-i-Imraan): is_attracted_to the joys of women and offspring [as well as wealth, horses, cattle and land] Mankind is created by Allah, and Allah knows what is in the heavens, the earth, and people’s breasts. In Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa): Mankind is a multitude that includes men and women. It is created by Allah from a single soul and receives Allah's bounty, though some are jealous of what Allah has bestowed upon it and would withhold even the speck on a date-stone if they shared in the ... In Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa): The male is an heir among brethren, men and women. When inheritance is divided, he receives a share equivalent to that of two females. In Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa): Man is described as created weak; therefore Allah would make the burden light for him. In Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa): Men are described as having earned fortunes, being in charge of women, and providing women financial support. In Surah 6: The Cattle (Al-An'aam): Humankind is appointed Allah’s viceroy of the earth, produced from a single being and guided through revelations and signs. In Surah 10: Jonas (Yunus): Mankind is warned, tested, and held accountable for its conduct. In Surah 11: Hud: Man is a created being who becomes despairing and thankless when mercy is withdrawn, then exultant and boastful when hardship is removed, except those who persevere and do good works. In Surah 13: The Thunder (Ar-Ra'd): Most of mankind do not believe despite the revelation and Allah's signs. In Surah 14: Abraham (Ibrahim): Mankind is called to leave darkness for light, heed the warnings, worship Allah, and reflect on His signs. In Surah 15: The Rock (Al-Hijr): Humanity is created from potter's clay of black mud altered. Allah then makes the mortal and breathes into him of His Spirit. In Surah 15: The Rock (Al-Hijr): Humanity is created from potter's clay of black mud altered, while the jinn are created from essential fire. In Surah 16: The Bee (An-Nahl): Mankind is created from a drop of fluid and given hearing, sight, and hearts. In Surah 17: The Night Journey (Al-Israa): Mankind is honored, carried on land and sea, and provided with good things. In Surah 21: The Prophets (Al-Anbiyaa): Mankind's reckoning draws near while many turn away heedlessly. In Surah 22: The Pilgrimage (Al-Hajj): Mankind is warned to fear Allah and is described as created through stages, brought forth as infants, and eventually dying or reaching an abject age. In The Believers (Al-Muminoon), man is formed through successive stages from earth and fluid, then dies and is raised again. In Surah 25: The Criterion (Al-Furqaan): Human beings are created from water and given kindred by blood and marriage. In Surah 29: The Spider (Al-Ankaboot): Mankind is commanded to worship Allah, show kindness to parents, seek provision from Allah, and travel the land to observe how creation began. In Surah 30: The Romans (Ar-Room), mankind is urged to reflect on Allah’s signs, follow upright religion, and avoid associating partners with Him, though most remain heedless or disbelieving. In Surah 31: Luqman: Mankind includes believers, disbelievers, those who dispute about Allah without knowledge, and those who follow inherited practices even when the devil invites them to the doom of flame. In Surah 32: The Prostration (As-Sajda): Mankind is included with the jinn among those whom Allah will fill hell with if they are evildoers. In Surah 35: The Originator (Faatir): Mankind is urged to remember Allah's grace, recognize that Allah alone provides for them, and avoid being deceived by worldly life or the beguiler. In Surah 36: Yaseen (Yaseen): Humanity is warned to worship Allah rather than the devil and is reminded of creation, death, resurrection, and accountability for deeds. In Surah 38: The letter Saad (Saad): All peoples are told that the revelation is a reminder for them. In Surah 39: The Groups (Az-Zumar): Human beings are created from one being, and Allah provides for them and creates them in their mothers' wombs. In Surah 39: The Groups (Az-Zumar): Mankind is addressed with revelation, warnings, promises, and similitudes. In Surah 41: Explained in detail (Fussilat): Humankind is warned, tested, and judged according to its deeds. In Surah 42: Consultation (Ash-Shura): Mankind is warned of the Day of assembling and urged to answer the call of the Lord before a day from Allah that cannot be averted. In Surah 46: The Dunes (Al-Ahqaf): Mankind will be gathered for judgment, when those worshipped besides Allah will become enemies of their worshippers and deny having been worshipped. In Surah 50: The letter Qaaf (Qaaf): Man was created by Allah, and Allah knows what his soul whispers to him and is nearer to him than his jugular vein. In Surah 51: The Winnowing Winds (Adh-Dhaariyat): Humankind were created by Allah only so that they might worship Him. In Surah 55: The Beneficent (Ar-Rahmaan): Man was created from clay like the potter's and was taught utterance. In Surah 56: The Inevitable (Al-Waaqia): Human beings are created by the Lord of the Worlds and are questioned about what they emit, cultivate, drink, and use to make fire. In Surah 65: Divorce (At-Talaaq): Men are instructed to put away women according to their legal period, reckon the period, keep their duty to Allah, provide lodging and maintenance, and either take women back or part from them kindly. In Surah 70: The Ascending Stairways (Al-Ma'aarij): Man was created anxious, fretful when evil befalls him, and grudging when good befalls him. In Surah 72: The Jinn (Al-Jinn): Humankind is mentioned as having been thought by the Jinn to speak no lie concerning Allah. In Surah 74: The Cloaked One (Al-Muddaththir): Men are warned by the signs of the Moon, the withdrawing night, and the shining dawn. In Surah 75: The Resurrection (Al-Qiyaama), man questions resurrection, loves the present life, neglects the Hereafter, and faces judgment for his conduct. In Surah 76: Man (Al-Insaan): Surah 76: Man (Al-Insaan) describes man's creation, guidance, duties, and final outcomes. In Surah 76: Man (Al-Insaan), man is created from thickened fluid, made hearing and knowing, shown the way, and given the choice to be grateful or disbelieving. In Surah 77: The Emissaries (Al-Mursalaat): They are created from a base fluid laid up in a safe abode for a known term. In Surah 78: The Announcement (An-Naba): They have been created in pairs, and their sleep has been appointed for repose. In Surah 80: He frowned (Abasa): He is described as self-destroyed and ungrateful, created from a drop of seed, proportioned, given an easy way, caused to die and be buried, and brought again to life when commanded. In Surah 83: Defrauding (Al-Mutaffifin): They will be raised again on the Awful Day and stand before the Lord of the Worlds. In Surah 89: The Dawn (Al-Fajr): Man says that his Lord honors him when he is tried through honor and ease, but says that his Lord despises him when his means of life are straitened. In The City (Al-Balad): Man is created in an atmosphere and is given two eyes, a tongue, and two lips. In Surah 95: The Fig (At-Tin): Man was created in the best stature and then reduced to the lowest of the low. In Surah 96: The Clot (Al-Alaq): Man is created from a clot and is taught by the pen what he did not know. In Surah 99: The Earthquake (Az-Zalzala): Man asks what ails Earth. Mankind issues forth in scattered groups to be shown their deeds. In Surah 101: The Calamity (Al-Qaari'a): Mankind will be as thickly-scattered moths on the day of the Calamity. In Surah 103: The Declining Day, Epoch (Al-Asr): He is described as being in a state of loss. In Surah 110: Divine Support (An-Nasr): Mankind enters the religion of Allah in troops when Allah's succour and the triumph come. In Surah 114: Mankind (An-Naas): Surah 114: Mankind (An-Naas) was revealed at Mecca. It tells mankind to seek refuge from the evil of the sneaking whisperer. In Surah 114: Mankind (An-Naas): Mankind is the subject of the Lord, King, and god named in the unit. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I: Mortal people, small beside gods, suffer through carelessness, labor, desire, violence, worldly attachment, and wealth; even their relations cannot ultimately protect them. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: Men are living beings who harm their souls and become wicked by hurting, killing, burning, or destroying beings and seeds for pleasure; they are born again as such beings, then die when their lives expire. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I, men are instructed in the Law but may neglect it through deceit and pride, or gain progress through self-control and obedience. In Sutrakritanga, Book II, men are people who may possess worldly goods or renounce them, commit sins, and receive rebirth or liberation according to their conduct. In So Dar and So Purukh (the Rahiras): Humanity is a helpless, God-forgetful creature entangled in worldly love, given a human body and roles in God’s play; it should join saints, repeat God’s name, meet God, and cross the terrible ocean. In Asa ki War (Asa di Var), men are mortal and morally accountable beings whose pride, ignorance, desire, and attachment can lead them to judgment or, through truth and God’s grace, freedom. In Asa ki War (Asa di Var), man is false when he loves falsehood but becomes true through truth, love of God, the Guru’s instruction, obedience, and service. In Hymns of Guru Nanak, man is a mortal creature created and sustained by God who must overcome attachment and forgetfulness through the Guru and God’s Name. In Hymns of Guru Nanak, men are portrayed as dishonest and attached to vice, yet capable of divine realization through the True One, the Guru, and God within. In Yasna: Men perform good or evil deeds and are urged to hear, observe, awaken, and choose rightly between two ways. In The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics: Men are a group who may come from beneath the sky to rest in the Tao once it has a name, becoming free from failure and error. In The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics: takes_law_from Earth Man takes his law from the Earth. In The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics, people are shaped by governance and social values, and can be guided toward simplicity and conformity through sage rule. In The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics: Men come forth and live, then enter again and die. Of every ten, three are ministers of life, three ministers of death, and three aim to live while moving toward the land of death. In The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics: The people suffer famine as superiors consume many taxes. Excessive agency by superiors makes them difficult to govern, while great labour to obtain living means leads them to make light of dying. In The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics: Man begins supple and weak at birth, then ends firm and strong at death. In Chuang Tzŭ, man proceeds from God and lives within Nature, but artificial distinctions, ambition, and reputation injure him; repose and inaction bring him toward TAO. In Chuang Tzŭ, man is a natural being born in TAO who becomes alienated through outward circumstances, civilization, and imposed conventions, and should return to natural order. In The Analects of Confucius, Book VI (Yung Yey): is_born_for uprightness Man is born for uprightness. If a man loses his uprightness and still lives, his survival is attributed to mere good fortune. In Mencius, Book I (Liang Hui Wang): The people suffer under rulers whose tours exhaust provisions and impose ceaseless toil, yet they share a ruler’s joy and sorrow when he shares their condition. In Mencius, Book II (Gong Sun Chou): Men universally possess four principles, commiseration, shame and dislike, modesty and complaisance, and approval and disapproval, which can fully develop into virtue; helping men practise virtue marks the superior man. In Mencius, Book II (Gong Sun Chou): Men submit sincerely when subdued by virtue, and their accord gives rise to a union stronger than Heaven’s opportunities or Earth’s advantages. In Mencius, Book IV (Li Lou): A man considers sincerity and character; his pupil reveals inner correctness through brightness or dullness. In Mencius, Book IV (Li Lou), the people prosper under benevolent and respectful rule but suffer from oppression, bad governance, excessive burdens, and contempt. In Mencius, Book IV (Li Lou), a man must examine and correct his own benevolence, wisdom, respect, and conduct before expecting recognition or submission from others. In Mencius, Book VI (Gaozi), men desire honour but possess truly honourable qualities that emerge when they are filled with benevolence and righteousness. In Mencius, Book VII (Jin Xin): A man follows his sense of righteousness, refraining from actions and desires it rejects; Chapter 17 presents this guidance as all he has to do. In Mencius, Book VII (Jin Xin): A man is given the families of Han and Wei. If he then looks upon himself without elation, he is far beyond the mass of men.

And if there be two sisters, then theirs are two-thirds of the heritage, and if they be brethren, men and women, unto the male is the equivalent of the share of two females. Allah expoundeth unto you, so that ye err not. Allah is Knower of all things.

In each work

man in each of the 99 accounts the graph joined, in canon order. Nothing is merged: an abstract, a narrative and a claim stay with the work they were written from.

man in The Book of Job

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man is portrayed as a frail, mortal creature: born of woman, living few days full of trouble, pain and mourning, like a flower cut down or a fleeting shadow. God fixes his days and months within bounds he cannot pass. He is unclean and cannot be righteous before God, a worm and son of man, less pure than the moon and stars. At death he lies down and does not rise, unaware of his sons' honor or lowliness. Yet he is capable of remarkable skill: mining ore, gems and metals, breaking shafts, overturning mountains and bringing hidden things to light. Still, he does not know wisdom's price, which is not found among the living. The account ends with God's answer that fear of the Lord is wisdom and turning from evil is understanding.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 14

Man is described as born of a woman, of few days, full of trouble, and like a flower cut down or a shadow that does not last. His days and months are determined by God, who has set bounds he cannot pass. When he dies he lies down and does not rise, and in death he does not know whether his sons are honored or brought low.

Chapter 25

Man is described as born of a woman and as a worm, the son of man. He is judged far less bright and pure than the moon and stars, which themselves fall short in God's sight.

Chapter 28

Man is shown as able to mine metals and gems, break open shafts, overturn mountains, and bring hidden things to light. Yet he does not know wisdom's price, and it is not found in the land of the living. God tells him that the fear of the Lord is wisdom and that departing from evil is understanding.

children of men in Psalms

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where children of men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Children of men, examined by the LORD looking down to find any who understand and seek God, are found all gone aside, together corrupt, with no one doing good.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 14

The LORD looks down on the children of men to see if any understand and seek God. They are found to have all gone aside and together become corrupt, with no one doing good, not one.

every man (humanity) in Psalms

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where every man (humanity) first appears

Abstract written by a model

Humanity is portrayed as fleeting: like a breath and a walking shadow, busying itself in vain and heaping up wealth for unknown heirs. Rebuked by the LORD for iniquity, its wealth is consumed like a moth; every man is but breath.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 39

Every man is described as standing as a breath and walking like a shadow, busying himself in vain. He heaps up wealth without knowing who will gather it, and when the LORD rebukes him for iniquity, his wealth is consumed like a moth. The text repeats that every man is but a breath.

man in Psalms

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man is the psalmist's subject of wonder: why the LORD cares for him. Made a little lower than the angels and crowned with glory and honor, he is then given rule over the LORD's works, with animals, birds, and sea creatures put under his feet.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 8

Man is the subject of the psalmist's wonder, as the question is asked why the LORD thinks of him and cares for him. He is made a little lower than the angels and crowned with glory and honor. He is made ruler over the works of the LORD's hands, with sheep, cattle, animals of the field, birds of the sky, and fish of the sea put under his feet.

sons of men in Ecclesiastes or, The Preacher

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where sons of men first appears

Abstract written by a model

The sons of men are portrayed as mortal creatures made from dust who share the animals' fate of death and hold no advantage over them. God tests them to show this. They bear heavy misery, lack power over the spirit and knowledge of the future, and cannot fully discover God's work however much they labor, though eternity is set in their hearts. Their hearts lean toward evil because sentence is delayed. The records move from this burden and limitation to the repeated counsel to rejoice, do good, eat, drink, and enjoy labor.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 3

The sons of men are given a burden by God and cannot fully discover his work, though eternity is set in their hearts. The narrator says it is best for them to rejoice, do good, and enjoy their labor. They are tested by God to see they are like animals, since they share the animals' fate and cannot know what will be after them.

Chapter 8

Man's misery is heavy on him, he does not know what will be, and he has no power over the spirit or the day of death. Hearts of the sons of men are set to do evil because sentence is not executed speedily. Man cannot find out the work done under the sun however much he labors, and has no better thing than to eat, drink, and be joyful.

man in Ecclesiastes or, The Preacher

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

"Man" is portrayed as a frail, limited creature whose life is vain and passes like a shadow. He labors for his mouth yet is never satisfied, cannot contend with one mightier than himself, and cannot know what comes after him or his own time. Made upright, he seeks many inventions, is sinful, and has a heart full of evil. He is likened to fish and birds suddenly snared by an evil time. The account moves from toil and vanity, through sin and ignorance, to death: all go to one place, the dead, and his everlasting home, with dust returning to earth and spirit to God. It ends with his whole duty: fear God and keep his commandments, since every work and hidden thing will be judged.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 6

Man's labor is all for his mouth, yet his appetite is never filled. He cannot contend with one mightier than he, his name was given long ago, and no one can tell him what is good for him or what will come after him. His life is vain and spent like a shadow.

Chapter 7

Mankind was made upright by God, but people search for many inventions. The text says all are subject to the sin and the cursing of others that the Preacher describes, and that people cannot find out what lies far off and deep.

Chapter 9

Man does not know whether love or hatred awaits him, nor does he know his time. He is compared to fish caught in an evil net and birds caught in a snare, snared suddenly by an evil time.

Chapter 12

Man is said to go to his everlasting home, with mourners going about the streets, and his dust returns to the earth while his spirit returns to God. The whole duty of man is to fear God and keep his commandments, and every work and hidden thing of his will be judged.

man (humanity created by God) in The Wisdom of Solomon

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where man (humanity created by God) first appears

Abstract written by a model

Humankind was formed by God's wisdom to rule creatures and the world in holiness and righteousness. Mortals have unstable thoughts, fragile plans and a burdensome corruptible body, and cannot grasp heavenly things; yet wisdom corrected their ways, taught them what pleases God, and saved them.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Chapter 9

Humankind was formed by God's wisdom to have dominion over the creatures and to rule the world in holiness, righteousness and uprightness. The text says even a perfect man counts for nothing without God's wisdom, that mortal thoughts are unstable and plans prone to fail, and that a corruptible body weighs down the soul. People can hardly guess earthly things and cannot trace heavenly ones, but through wisdom their ways were corrected, they were taught what pleases God, and they were saved.

Mankind is made from earth and returns to it, formed in the Lord's image with numbered days. Humans hold authority over beasts and birds and are given counsel, senses, and knowledge of good and evil. They received a law of life and a covenant, are commanded to beware of unrighteousness and to treat their neighbors rightly, and are urged to praise the Lord while alive. They are shown as limited and mortal, not capable of everything, prone to devising evil, and ultimately earth and ashes.

mankind in Surah 2: The Cow (Al-Baqara)

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where mankind first appears

Abstract written by a model

Mankind is Allah’s creation, called to worship Him and avoid rivals. Originally one community, mankind receives guidance through the messenger, prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners, and Scripture with the truth. It includes believers, disbelievers, and those who claim belief while acting otherwise; some oppose, ask questions, or face affliction. Mankind is made a witness through a middle nation and instructed to follow guidance, eat what is lawful and wholesome, ward off evil, and seek good in this world and the Hereafter.

Mankind is called to worship Allah, who created them and those before them, and to avoid setting up rivals to Him. Among mankind are believers, disbelievers, and people who claim belief while acting otherwise.

mankind in Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa)

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where mankind first appears

Abstract written by a model

Mankind is a multitude that includes men and women. It is created by Allah from a single soul and receives Allah's bounty, though some are jealous of what Allah has bestowed upon it and would withhold even the speck on a date-stone if they shared in the Sovereignty. Mankind is told to be careful of its duty toward Allah, the wombs, and claims of rights over one another, and to speak kindly and justly. Muhammad is instructed to judge between mankind by the truth Allah shows in the Scripture. Messengers are sent so that mankind will have no argument against Allah after them. At the close, mankind is told that the messenger has come with Truth from its Lord and is urged to believe, because belief is better for it; even if it disbelieves, whatever is in the heavens and earth belongs to Allah, the Knower and Wise.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa) / 1 O mankind! Be careful of your duty to your Lord Who created you from a single soul and .. 10 Lo! Those who devour the wealth of orphans wrongfully, they do but swallow fire into their bellies, and they will be exposed to burning flame.

Mankind is told to be careful of its duty toward Allah, toward the wombs, and in claims of rights over one another. Mankind is also instructed to speak kindly and justly.

43 O ye who believe! Draw not near unto prayer when ye are drunken, till ye know that which ye utter, nor when ye are polluted, save when journeying upon the road, till ye have bathed. And if ye be ill, or on a journey, or one of you cometh from the closet, or ye have touched women, and ye find not water, then go to high clean soil and rub your faces and your hands (therewith). Lo! Allah is Benign, Forgiving. .. 57 And as for those who believe and do good works, We shall make them enter Gardens underneath which rivers flow - to dwell therein for ever; there for them are pure companions - and We shall make them enter plenteous shade.

Some are described as being jealous of mankind because of what Allah bestowed upon them. The text says that if certain people had a share in the Sovereignty, they would not give mankind even the speck on a date-stone.

101 And when ye go forth in the land, it is no sin for you to curtail (your) worship if ye fear that those who disbelieve may attack you. In truth the disbelievers are an open enemy to you. .. 105 Lo! We reveal unto thee the Scripture with the truth, that thou mayst judge between mankind by that which Allah show

Mankind is the group between whom Muhammad is instructed to judge by the truth shown to him in the Scripture.

153 The people of the Scripture ask of thee that thou shouldst cause an (actual) Book to descend upon them from heaven. They asked a greater thing of Moses aforetime, for they said: Show us Allah plainly. The storm of lightning seized them for their wickedness. Then (even) after that) they chose the calf (for worship) after clear proofs (of Allah's Sovereignty) had come unto them. And We forgave them that! And We bestowed on Moses evident authority. .. 168 Lo! those who disbelieve and deal in wrong, Allah will never forgive them, neither will He guide them unto a road, /

The messengers are sent so that mankind might have no argument against Allah after the messengers.

170 O mankind! The messenger hath come unto you with the Truth from your Lord. Therefor believe; (it is) better for you. But if ye disbelieve, still, lo! unto Allah belongeth whatsoever is in the heavens and the earth. Allah is ever Knower, Wise. .. 176 They ask thee for a pronouncement. Say: Allah hath pronounced for you concerning distant kindred. If a man die childless and he have a sister, hers is half the heritage, and he would have inherited from her had she died childless. And if there be two sisters, then theirs are two-thirds of the heritage, and if they be brethren, men and women, unto the male is the equivalent of the share of two females. Allah expoundeth unto you, so that ye err not. Allah is Knower of all things.

Mankind is told that the messenger has come with the Truth from Allah and is urged to believe. Mankind is also told that Allah owns whatever is in the heavens and the earth.

the male in Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa)

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where the male first appears

Abstract written by a model

The male is an heir among brethren, men and women. When inheritance is divided, he receives a share equivalent to that of two females. This rule appears within a pronouncement concerning distant kindred and childless deaths, where sisters’ shares are also specified, and concludes with Allah’s explanation so that people do not err.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

170 O mankind! The messenger hath come unto you with the Truth from your Lord. Therefor believe; (it is) better for you. But if ye disbelieve, still, lo! unto Allah belongeth whatsoever is in the heavens and the earth. Allah is ever Knower, Wise. .. 176 They ask thee for a pronouncement. Say: Allah hath pronounced for you concerning distant kindred. If a man die childless and he have a sister, hers is half the heritage, and he would have inherited from her had she died childless. And if there be two sisters, then theirs are two-thirds of the heritage, and if they be brethren, men and women, unto the male is the equivalent of the share of two females. Allah expoundeth unto you, so that ye err not. Allah is Knower of all things.

The male receives the equivalent of the share of two females when there are brethren, men and women.

  1. “to the male the equivalent of the portion of two females”Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa) · 11 Allah chargeth you concerning (the provision for) your children: to the male the equivalent of the portion of two females, and if there be women more than two, then theirs is two-thirds of the inheritance, and if there be one (only) then the half. And to each of his parents a sixth of the inheritance, if he have a son; and if he have no son and his parents are his heirs, then to his mother appertaineth the third; and if he have brethren, then to his mother appertaineth the sixth, after any legacy he may have bequeathed, or debt (hath been paid). Your parents and your children: Ye know not which of them is nearer unto you in usefulness. It is an injunction from Allah. Lo! Allah is Knower, Wise. .. 14 And whoso disobeyeth Allah and His messenger and transgresseth His limits, He will make him enter Fire, where he will dwell for ever; his will be a shameful doom.

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man is described as created weak; therefore Allah would make the burden light for him. In inheritance, as a husband, he receives one-half if his wife has no child. He is also addressed in rules forbidding forcible inheritance of women and requiring kind consort.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

19 O ye who believe! It is not lawful for you forcibly to inherit the women (of your deceased kinsmen), nor (that) ye should put constraint upon them that ye may take away a part of that which ye have given them, unless they be guilty of flagrant lewdness. But consort with them in kindness, for if ye hate them it may happen that ye hate a thing wherein Allah hath placed much good. .. 28 Allah would make the burden light for you, for man was created weak.

Man was created weak, and Allah therefore would make the burden light for him.

men in Surah 4: The Women (An-Nisaa)

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Men are described as having earned fortunes, being in charge of women, and providing women financial support. Their authority is attributed to Allah making some excel others and to their spending of property for women’s support. Men may also be feeble and unable to devise a plan or find a way. The records culminate by restating their described authority and financial role.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

29 O ye who believe! Squander not your wealth among yourselves in vanity, except it be a trade by mutual consent, and kill not one another. Lo! Allah is ever Merciful unto you. .. 35 And if ye fear a breach between them twain (the man and wife), appoint an arbiter from his folk and an arbiter from her folk. If they desire amendment Allah will make them of one mind. Lo! Allah is ever Knower, Aware.

Men are described as being in charge of women because Allah has made some of them excel others and because men spend of their property for women's support.

men in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Men are taught the Law and instructed in conduct, yet may neglect it through rude speech, false explanations, untruth, concealment, quarrelsomeness, deceit, pride, and attachment to sensual objects. They are urged instead to control themselves, obey their teacher, and conform impartially to admonitions, becoming beyond deceit. False conduct and seeking honour through knowledge, birth, or austerities bring harm or continued rebirth.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Thirteenth Lecture, Called the Real Truth.

Men are taught to distinguish good conduct from wickedness, to control themselves, and to avoid deceit, pride, quarrelsomeness, greed, and attachment to sensual objects. Those who act falsely or use knowledge, birth, or austerities for honour suffer harm or continued rebirth.

men in Sutrakritanga, Book II

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Men are people found in the East, West, North, and South, including Âryas, non-Âryas, and barbarians. They may own houses, fields, servants, and peasants, or renounce possessions and lead a mendicant's life. They are born in Karmabhûmi, Akarmabhûmi, or minor continents; cruel, proud men accompanied only by Karman are born in hell while causing others to suffer and die. Men may commit sins through motives, accident, error, lying, theft, passions, pride, mistreatment, deceit, and greed; some practise occult sciences or become agents of harm. Their degree of cessation from sin places them in demerit, merit, or a mixed state, determining suffering in hell and Samsâra, liberation, or rebirth as gods. Human beings arise in a womb and progress from parental substances to milk and solid foods.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Second Lecture, Called on Activity.

Men commit sins for personal or other motives, without personal interest, by accident, through error of sight, lying, theft, mental passions, pride, mistreatment, deceit, and greed. Some men practise occult sciences or become companions, helpers, burglars, hunters, fishermen, cowherds, animal slayers, and other agents of harm, thereby degrading themselves.

Now the explanation of the second subject, viz. merit, is as follows:

Men are described as belonging to demerit, merit, or the mixed state according to whether they practise cessation from sin completely, not at all, or only in part. Their conduct determines whether they suffer in hell and Samsâra, attain liberation, or are reborn as gods.

Third Lecture, Called Knowledge of Food.

Human beings arise in a womb through the combination of a man and a woman and the deposit of their humours. They first feed on menses and semen, later absorb the essence of the mothers’ food, suck milk as babies, and afterward eat boiled rice, gruel, and movable or immovable beings.

Claims worded by a model

humanity in So Dar and So Purukh (the Rahiras)

The Sikh Religion · the work’s page · read it where humanity first appears

Abstract written by a model

Humanity is a helpless, God-forgetful creature entangled in worldly love, given a human body and roles in God’s play; it should join saints, repeat God’s name, meet God, and cross the terrible ocean.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

So Purukh

Humanity is portrayed as helpless, forgetful, spiritually weak, and prone to drowning in worldly love. Human beings receive different roles as givers and beggars within God's wondrous play. Guru Arjan teaches that humanity should use the human body to meet God, join the saints, repeat God's name, and prepare for the crossing.

men in Asa ki War (Asa di Var)

Guru Nanak · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

The records portray men as mortal, morally accountable beings whose lives are often ruled by pride, ignorance, desire, and attachment to worldly pleasure. A man may enjoy himself, gain wealth or greatness, live in mansions and palaces, display wealth, exercise authority, and fix his heart on horses and women, yet he forgets death and ultimately becomes ashes. Death takes even the great and wealthy; afterward comes judgment, an account of acts, suffering for one’s own acts, and no shelter for the condemned. Pride makes men true or false, leads them toward hell or heaven, blinds them, and leaves them without deliverance or salvation. Religious exertions, pilgrimages, garbs, fasting, silence, hardship, or dwelling in wilderness and cremation-grounds do not by themselves remove this burden. Some men do not know their origin and hold excessive opinions of themselves; in the blackness of the age they lack divine knowledge and may become demons. Yet the course can change: desire for and abiding in truth, meeting the true Guru, fixing God’s name in the heart, being favoured by God, and destroying pride through the Word bring freedom from hopes and fears and reveal God’s gate.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Slok III .. Pauri III

Man enjoys himself but eventually becomes ashes. However wealthy or great he is, ministers of Death take him away, and a judgment of his acts follows; the condemned finds no shelter and no one hears his cries.

Slok VII .. Pauri VII

Man is born, dies, gives, takes, earns, spends, rejoices, mourns, and becomes true or false in pride. Man may go to hell or heaven according to pride, but when pride is removed, God's gate is seen. Without divine knowledge, man troubles himself by talking, and as he regards God, God regards him.

Slok IX .. Pauri IX

Man may read books for his whole life, wander on pilgrimages, wear religious garbs, avoid corn or clothes, remain silent, sleep in ignorance, go barefoot, eat filth, or dwell in wilderness and burial and cremation-grounds, yet he still suffers for his own acts. Man obtains comfort only by meeting the true Guru and fixing God's name in his heart.

Slok XVII .. Pauri XVII

Men fix their hearts on caparisoned horses and adorned women, live in mansions, pavilions, and palaces, and enjoy pleasures. They exercise authority and forget death, but they fail because they do not know God, and their youth will fail when old age comes.

man in Asa ki War (Asa di Var)

Guru Nanak · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man is false when falsehood is in him: he loves falsehood and forgets God. With truth in his heart, he is known as true, loves God, knows the true way, receives true instruction, shows mercy, and performs charity. He inquires of the true Guru, then rests in his own heart. By obeying God’s order and serving the Master, he becomes acceptable to the Lord, reaches God’s court, gains his heart’s desire and a robe of honour, and attains salvation through enraptured hearing of the Name.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Slok X .. Pauri X

Man becomes true when truth is in his heart, falsehood departs, and he loves the True One. He is urged to know the true way, show mercy, perform charity, seek instruction from the true Guru, meditate on the Unseen One, and serve according to what has been allotted.

man in Hymns of Guru Nanak

Guru Nanak · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man is a mortal, perishable trader in the world, with a body of blood, semen, wind, water, and fire, and a soul, breath, and body belonging to God. Created, sustained, and sheltered by God, he is born according to the heart’s desires and is subject from the beginning to destiny, death, and God’s account. His life is brief and uncertain; when its measure is full, death summons him, examines his accounts, and his actions bring their own reward and fruit. Worldly possessions cannot accompany him. Man’s central danger is forgetfulness of God. Attachment, passion, greed, pride, falsehood, pleasure, status, ritual, possessions, and varied phases and directions of life lead him astray. He forsakes ambrosia for poison, accumulates demerits, becomes entangled, and, without the Guru or God’s Name, suffers wandering and transmigration after an unprofitable death. Religious garb, pilgrimage, and austerity do not bring comfort without the Name. The records urge man to subdue pride, adopt virtues, practise truth, serve God and the true Guru, meditate on and repeat God’s Name, and love the True One. Through God’s favour, the Name, the Guru’s instruction, service, virtue, and becoming the holy man’s slave, he obtains happiness, honour, salvation, emancipation, and rescue. Humanity is also taught that God’s light is in every heart and all people are equal.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

7 sections; each heading opens the text

Sri Rag .. Ashtapadi

Man is urged to serve God, meditate on God's name, practice truth and virtue, and take refuge in the Guru. Man is temporary, subject to death and transmigration, and cannot carry worldly possessions beyond this life.

Rag Gauri .. Gauri Chhant

The human being is born and absorbed according to the desires of the heart and is judged according to acts. Human life is precious but is wasted through sleep, eating, greed, pride, falsehood, and attachment to possessions.

Rag Asa .. Asa Ashtapadi

Man is created and sustained by God, but suffers through ignorance, pride, worldly attachment, greed, and reliance on ritual or status. Man is accepted, forgiven, and saved by God’s favour when he follows the Guru, remembers the Name, practises truth and devotion, and abandons worldly love.

Rag Sorath .. Sorath Ashtapadi

Humanity is subject to death, destiny, sin, greed, falsehood, and transmigration, but can obtain happiness and liberation through God's Name, the Guru, virtue, and service. Humanity is taught to regard all people as equal because God's light is in every heart.

Rag Dhanasari .. Dhanasari Chhant

Humanity lives only briefly and does not know how long life will last. People are taught to serve God, remember Him, act truthfully, and understand that their actions determine their reward.

Rag Suhi .. Suhi ki War

Man wanders through attachment, passion, falsehood, pleasure, and transmigration, often forgetting the Creator. He is saved by service, love for the True One, the Name, the Word, good fortune, and the Guru's instruction.

Bhairo

Man dies an unprofitable death and wanders in transmigration without God's name. He may adopt religious practices, pilgrimage, austerities, or devotional dress, but he will not find comfort or satisfaction without the Name.

men in Hymns of Guru Nanak

Guru Nanak · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Men are depicted as dishonourable and dishonest, consuming carrion, speaking falsehood, and gaining an evil reputation after death. Yet the human body and heart can house divine realization. Humanity is warned against vice and attachment, but may find freedom through recognizing the True One, the Guru’s instruction, and God within.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Rag Malar .. Malar ki War

Humanity is warned against coveting others' wives and goods and against pride, evil inclinations, slander, lust, wrath, and worldly attachment. People are said to be subject to God's order and fate, but can find freedom by recognizing the True One, receiving the Guru's instruction, and finding God in their hearts.

men in Yasna

L.H. Mills · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Men perform good or evil deeds and are urged to hear, observe, awaken, and choose rightly between two ways. Rival forces deceive them, threatening happy life and Immortality, while wickedness brings long wounding and righteousness brings blessings.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Yasna XXX.

It is urged to hear, observe, awaken to the teaching, and choose rightly between the two ways. It is also taught about the long wounding of the wicked and the blessings of the righteous.

Yasna XXXII.

Mankind is threatened with deception, loss of happy life on earth, and loss of Immortality. The rival forces and their teachings are said to confuse and beguile mankind.

men in The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics

Lao-Tse · the work’s page · read it where men first appears

Abstract written by a model

Men are a group who may come from beneath the sky to rest in the Tao once it has a name, becoming free from failure and error. They dislike being orphans, having little virtue, and carriages without naves. They are taught that increase and diminution can reverse one another. In governing, they are not hurt by departed spirits, yet they do not know the originating principle or authoritative law, and therefore do not know the speaker.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

42. 1. The Tao produced One; One produced Two; Two produced Three;

Men dislike being orphans, having little virtue, and being as carriages without naves. Men are taught that some things are increased by being diminished and others are diminished by being increased.

60. 1. Governing a great state is like cooking small fish.

Men are not hurt by the spiritual energy of the manes of the departed, and the ruling sage does not hurt them.

70. 1. My words are very easy to know, and very easy to practise; but

Men do not know the originating and all-comprehending principle or the authoritative law, and because they do not know these, they do not know the speaker.

people in The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics

Lao-Tse · the work’s page · read it where people first appears

Abstract written by a model

The people are portrayed as a body whose condition depends strongly on governance and social values. Rivalry, theft, robbery, and disorder arise when superior ability is valued, scarce goods are prized, or contrivance and scheming for gain prevail. The sage’s rule prevents these conditions by emptying minds, filling bellies, weakening wills, strengthening bones, and withholding instruments of state profit. The people are to be loved without purposeful action, yet sages deal with them as dogs of grass. In the highest antiquity they did not know their rulers; later they loved and praised them, then feared them, and finally despised them, eventually claiming to be as they were of themselves. They would benefit greatly from abandoning sageness and wisdom, and would become filial and kindly by renouncing benevolence and righteousness. Their mind is made the sage’s mind; they direct eyes and ears toward him and become good and sincere through his treatment. They love byways despite the level, easy Great Tao, live amid intertwined misery and happiness, and remain long deluded about correction and distortion. They often ruin affairs at the point of success and are difficult to govern when knowledgeable; simplicity, ignorance, and mysterious excellence lead them to conformity.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

11 sections; each heading opens the text

3. 1. Not to value and employ men of superior ability is the way to

The people are kept from rivalry, theft, and disorder through the sage's governance. The sage empties their minds, fills their bellies, weakens their wills, and strengthens their bones.

The people are dealt with by the sages as dogs of grass are dealt with.

The people are to be loved without proceeding through purposeful action.

The people passed through successive attitudes toward their rulers: they first did not know them, then loved and praised them, then feared them, and finally despised them. They later said that they were as they were of themselves.

19. 1. If we could renounce our sageness and discard our wisdom, it

The people would benefit greatly if sageness and wisdom were renounced. They would again become filial and kindly if benevolence and righteousness were discarded.

36. 1. When one is about to take an inspiration, he is sure to make a

The people should not be shown instruments for the profit of a state.

49. 1. The sage has no invariable mind of his own; he makes the mind

The people’s mind becomes the sage’s mind. They direct their eyes and ears toward the sage, and through his treatment of them, all become good and sincere.

53. 1. If I were suddenly to become known, and (put into a position

People love the byways even though the Great Tao is level and easy.

58.

The people are subject to changing conditions in which misery and happiness accompany or conceal one another. Their delusion about correction and distortion has persisted for a long time.

64. 1. That which is at rest is easily kept hold of; before a thing

People in their conduct of affairs are constantly ruining them when they are on the eve of success.

65. 1. The ancients who showed their skill in practising the Tao did

The people are difficult to govern when they have much knowledge. They are made simple and ignorant by those who practise the Tao, and mysterious excellence leads them to conformity with its possessor.

people in The Tao Teh King, or The Tao and Its Characteristics

Lao-Tse · the work’s page · read it where people first appears

Abstract written by a model

The people suffer famine as superiors consume many taxes. Excessive agency by superiors makes them difficult to govern, while great labour to obtain living means leads them to make light of dying.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

75. 1. The people suffer from famine because of the multitude of taxes

The people suffer from famine because of the multitude of taxes consumed by their superiors. They are difficult to govern because of the excessive agency of their superiors, and they make light of dying because of the greatness of their labours in seeking the means of living.

Man in Chuang Tzŭ

Chuang Tzŭ · the work’s page · read it where Man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man proceeds from God, should express the divine on earth, and lives as habitat for human beings within Nature’s inescapable operation. He has natural instincts that once supported quiet, free, integrated life through clothing and cultivation. Under the Sages, knowledge and gain brought struggle. Man’s heart and will, rather than Nature, cause injury through artificial distinctions, emotion, ambition, and reputation. He may approach TAO through repose, necessity, inaction, and freedom from artificiality while recognizing the known and unknown.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

CHAPTER VI.

Man proceeds from God and should express on earth what God is for eternity in the universe. Man must recognize both the known and the unknown while working out his allotted span.

CHAPTER IX.

The people have natural instincts to weave and clothe themselves and to till and feed themselves. In the days when natural instincts prevailed, they lived quietly, moved freely, and were naturally integrated; under the Sages, they became interested in knowledge and struggled for gain.

CHAPTER XXIII.

Man's will is called the most deadly weapon, and man's heart rather than Nature is said to cause injury. Man becomes confused by artificial distinctions, emotional disturbances, ambition, and the desire for reputation or pre-eminence. He can approach TAO by cultivating repose, adapting to necessity, and freeing himself from artificiality.

Claims worded by a model

  1. “The people have certain natural instincts;--to weave and clothe themselves, to till and feed themselves.”Chuang Tzŭ · CHAPTER IX.

  1. “Man's life is as the soot on a kettle.”Chuang Tzŭ · CHAPTER XXIII.

man in Chuang Tzŭ

Chuang Tzŭ · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

Man is a two-footed flesh-eating being, born in TAO, with natural dispositions, senses, instincts, and inborn skill. He can live peacefully through TAO and natural order, but is controlled by outward circumstances and can become unmanageable in fury. His senses and intelligence obscure his spiritual nature; social conventions, government, moral reform, intellectualism, art, and learning scatter his original integrity. As he applies skill to civilized life, discards instinct, and follows the majority slavishly, he becomes unable to return to his original condition. He should avoid coercive rule and imposed superiority, rely on passive virtue, cultivate his own nature, and advance by becoming unconscious of objective existences.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

CHAPTER IV.

He is portrayed as manageable when properly managed but unmanageable when excited to fury. He is advised to rely on passive virtue and to avoid imposing superiority on others.

Nan Po Tzŭ K'uei

Human beings are described as being born in TAO and as needing TAO in order to live peacefully. They are also portrayed as subject to dreams, mortality, social conventions, and transformations of life and death.

CHAPTER VII.

It should not be governed through coercive laws and regulations, but allowed to follow natural order.

CHAPTER XI.

Mankind loses steadiness when excessive joy and sorrow displace its centre of gravity. Mankind is also harmed when rulers try to manage people as if they were mere things.

CHAPTER XII.

Man is controlled by outward circumstances and applies in-born skill to civilized life, but his senses and intelligence can obscure his spiritual nature. Man advances toward the divine by becoming unconscious of objective existences and cultivating his own nature.

CHAPTER XVI.

Man's original integrity was scattered by systems of government, moral reform, goodness, intellectualism, art, and learning. He became unable to return to his natural instincts and original condition.

the people in Mencius, Book I (Liang Hui Wang)

Mencius · the work’s page · read it where the people first appears

Abstract written by a model

The people suffer under rulers whose tours exhaust provisions and impose ceaseless toil, yet they share a ruler’s joy and sorrow when he shares their condition. They should be enabled to gratify wealth and beauty; king Wen advanced welfare through provisions, protection, and care for the destitute.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 4

The people suffer when rulers' tours consume provisions, deprive the hungry of food, and force people to toil without rest. They rejoice in a ruler's joy and grieve at his sorrow when the ruler shares their condition.

Chapter 5

The people are to be enabled to satisfy their feelings for wealth and beauty. Under king Wen's government, their welfare was advanced through provisions, protection, and attention to the destitute.

people in Mencius, Book IV (Li Lou)

Mencius · the work’s page · read it where people first appears

Abstract written by a model

The people receive no benefit from benevolent intentions unless ancient ways are practiced, and suffer when oppression and wickedness come from high stations. Their hearts are central: gaining them requires collecting what they like and avoiding what they dislike, and they turn to benevolent rule as water flows downward. They may be injured by extreme oppression, governed unsuccessfully by inferiors lacking sovereign confidence, burdened with doubled grain payments and forced cultivation, or despised and plundered by fearful princes. Respectful and economical rulers neither despise nor plunder them.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Mencius, Book IV (Li Lou) / Part I / Chapter 1

The people receive no benefits when princes merely possess benevolent intentions without putting the ways of the ancient kings into practice. They suffer when wickedness is disseminated from high stations.

Chapter 2

A ruler should rule the people as Yao ruled his. A ruler who carries oppression of the people to the highest pitch injures them.

Chapter 9

The people are gained when their hearts are gained, which requires collecting what they like and not imposing what they dislike. The people turn toward a benevolent rule as water flows downward and wild beasts fly to the wilderness.

Chapter 12

The people cannot be successfully governed by those occupying inferior situations unless those persons obtain the confidence of the sovereign.

Chapter 14

The people were made to pay double the grain formerly paid by Qiu. They could also be compelled to cultivate ground taken into grassy commons.

Chapter 16

People may be despised or plundered by a prince who fears they will not obey him. They are also the others whom the respectful do not despise and the economical do not plunder.

man in Mencius, Book IV (Li Lou)

Mencius · the work’s page · read it where man first appears

Abstract written by a model

A man responds to failed attachment, rule, or politeness by examining his own benevolence, wisdom, and feeling of respect. He has a correct person, studies to remain in harmony with the ordinances of God, and obtains much happiness. His self-conduct precedes others’ recognition, submission, or contempt: when his person is correct, the whole kingdom turns to him; he must first despise himself before others despise him.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter 4

A man is told to examine his own benevolence, wisdom, and feeling of respect when his actions do not produce the desired response. When his person is correct, the whole kingdom will turn to him with recognition and submission.

Chapter 8

A man must first despise himself before others will despise him. His own conduct is presented as preceding the contempt he receives from others.
